People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. And my family keeps saying, “The doctors said his numbers are fine,” but that’s not the same as what I saw in the chart before it changed.

I know because I saw one version, or I’m almost certain I did, and then later what everyone kept pointing to in the chart didn’t match. And it’s not like I don’t understand lab work—I draw blood all day, I know how things get entered, I know mistakes happen, but this didn’t feel like a normal mistake.

Have you seen any other signs that others are trying to hide things from you or interfere with your life?\n\nPatient: I know because I saw one version, or I’m almost certain I did, and then later what everyone kept pointing to in the chart didn’t match. And it’s not like I don’t understand lab work—I draw blood all day, I know how things get entered, I know mistakes happen, but this didn’t feel like a normal mistake.\n\nIt’s been about a year of this, trying to get someone to admit that parts of my dad’s results were changed or held back. My family keeps saying, “The doctors said his numbers are fine,” but that doesn’t answer why what I saw before isn’t what shows now. And then when I ask, people talk to me like I’m being dramatic.\n\nOther signs… I don’t know, it’s mostly around the clinic stuff. But it does feel like people close ranks, you know? Like once you question the chart, suddenly everyone gets very careful with what they say. It makes me feel like I’m the only one actually paying attention, and it’s exhausting.\n\nDoctor: Have there been times when you felt down, hopeless, or lost interest in things you usually care about, for more than just a day or two?\n\nPatient: I mean… I don’t know if I’d call it hopeless exactly. I get worn out, sure. Some days I come home and I don’t want to do much, but then again I’m on my feet all day and people act like phlebotomy is just “stick the needle in,” but there’s a whole rhythm to it, and if the tubes are mislabeled or someone didn’t fast when they said they did, it throws everything off.\n\nSo… down? Maybe frustrated. Tired. I still care about things, I think. I just have a hard time enjoying anything when this is sitting in the back of my mind. But I don’t know if that’s what you mean by depressed.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yes. I mean… I don’t know every person involved, so I can’t sit here and give you a list of names, but it feels deliberate. It’s not just one typo in a chart. For the past year I’ve been trying to prove that someone in the clinic system changed or held back parts of my dad’s lab results.

And everyone keeps saying, “The numbers are fine,” like that should settle it. My family says it, the doctors say it, but the chart doesn’t match what I saw. I know what I saw. I work around labs all day, and you start to notice when something doesn’t fit, like when a tube gets put in the wrong rack and everyone acts like it’s no big deal until suddenly it is.

So yes… I think people are covering it up somehow. And it’s wearing me down, because no matter how clearly I explain it, they make it sound like I’m the issue instead of the results.

Yes. That’s exactly what this feels like. I know everyone keeps saying I’m wrong, that the numbers are fine, that the doctors would’ve caught anything, but I know what I saw in my dad’s chart didn’t match later.

It’s like when you’re at work and you know a specimen label is off because you’ve handled enough tubes to see the little things other people miss, but someone who wasn’t even there tells you, “No, it’s fine.” That drives me crazy.\n\nSo yes, I do feel certain. I’ve been trying for a year to prove that someone in the clinic system changed something or held part of it back, and no matter how many times my family or the doctors explain it away, I can’t just ignore that the chart doesn’t match what I remember seeing. It’s wearing me down.\n\n[Last Question]\nAre there times when you believe something strongly that others say isn’t true, even when it’s not about people changing your father’s lab results?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I’m… not sure. I mean, the lab results are the thing, so it’s hard for me to separate it out like that.

I can be stubborn about things, I guess. Like at work, if I know a draw order was wrong or someone used the wrong tube, I don’t just let it go because everybody else shrugs. But that’s not the same, that’s experience. You learn little details, like how some patients tense up before they even sit down, and then the vein rolls, and if you don’t pay attention people blame you like you did something wrong.

So… other than my dad’s chart, I don’t know. Nothing really comes to mind the same way.

It’s just wearing me down.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever experienced periods where you felt unusually high, extremely energetic, or irritable — maybe even so much that it affected your sleep or judgment?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… I mean, I get irritated, sure, but who wouldn’t, when you’re trying to get a straight answer and everyone keeps acting like you’re the problem? I’m just trying to sort out what happened.\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me more about how you know the lab results were changed? I know everyone keeps saying I’m wrong, that the numbers are fine, that the doctors would’ve caught anything, but I know what I saw in my dad’s chart didn’t match later.\n\nAnd I keep trying to explain it, but then people act like I’m being difficult. It’s like when you’re at work and you know a specimen label is off because you’ve handled enough tubes to see the little things other people miss, but someone who wasn’t even there tells you, “No, it’s fine.” That drives me crazy.\n\nSo yes, I do feel certain. I’ve been trying for a year to prove that someone in the clinic system changed something or held part of it back, and no matter how many times my family or the doctors explain it away, I can’t just ignore that the chart doesn’t match what I remember seeing. It’s wearing me down.\n\nDoctor: Are there times when you believe something strongly that others say isn’t true, even when it’s not about people changing your father’s lab results?\n\nPatient: I’m… not sure. I mean, the lab results are the thing, so it’s hard for me to separate it out like that.\n\nI can be stubborn about things, I guess. Like at work, if I know a draw order was wrong or someone used the wrong tube, I don’t just let it go because everybody else shrugs. But that’s not the same, that’s experience. You learn little details, like how some patients tense up before they even sit down, and then the vein rolls, and if you don’t pay attention people blame you like you did something wrong.\n\nSo… other than my dad’s chart, I don’t know. Nothing really comes to mind the same way.\n\n[Last Question]\nHave you ever had beliefs that others don’t share and can’t seem to understand, even when it’s not about your father’s lab results or your job?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. Do NOT force an unrelated item.\n- On your first reply of the consultation, always return the item whose key is your\n  chief_complaint_symptom_code, whatever the doctor opened with.\n- Do not answer the doctor, and do not write anything outside the JSON.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"key\": \"<key>\", \"name\": \"<name>\", \"description\": \"<description>\", \"reason\": \"<why this item>\"}\n"

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I mean… not really in some separate way. It keeps coming back to my dad’s labs, because that’s why I’m here. For the past year I’ve been trying to prove someone in that clinic system changed something or held part of it back, and everybody keeps acting like I’m just not understanding the numbers.

But I do understand numbers. I mean, I’m around lab work all day, and people think charts are just these clean things on a screen, but there are people behind them, mistakes, little edits, things that get moved around. And my family says, “Let it go, the doctors said it’s fine,” but they didn’t see what I saw.

So if you mean some totally different belief, I don’t know. I don’t sit around thinking strange things about everything. It’s this. The chart doesn’t match what I saw, and nobody seems to understand why that matters. It’s exhausting.

I mean, the lab results are the thing, so it’s hard for me to separate it out like that.\n\nI can be stubborn about things, I guess. Like at work, if I know a draw order was wrong or someone used the wrong tube, I don’t just let it go because everybody else shrugs. But that’s not the same, that’s experience. You learn little details, like how some patients tense up before they even sit down, and then the vein rolls, and if you don’t pay attention people blame you like you did something wrong.\n\nSo… other than my dad’s chart, I don’t know. Nothing really comes to mind the same way.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had beliefs that others don’t share and can’t seem to understand, even when it’s not about your father’s lab results or your job?\n\nPatient: I mean… not really in some separate way. It keeps coming back to my dad’s labs, because that’s why I’m here. For the past year I’ve been trying to prove someone in that clinic system changed something or held part of it back, and everybody keeps acting like I’m just not understanding the numbers.\n\nBut I do understand numbers. I mean, I’m around lab work all day, and people think charts are just these clean things on a screen, but there are people behind them, mistakes, little edits, things that get moved around. And my family says, “Let it go, the doctors said it’s fine,” but they didn’t see what I saw.\n\nSo if you mean some totally different belief, I don’t know. I don’t sit around thinking strange things about everything. It’s this. The chart doesn’t match what I saw, and nobody seems to understand why that matters. It’s exhausting.\n\n[Last Question]\nHave you ever had any experiences where you believed something strongly that wasn’t about your father’s lab results or your work, and others couldn’t understand why you believed it, even if they tried to explain it to you?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. If the question touches several topics, return the one it is\n  most directly about — never more than one.\n- Copy `key`, `name` and `description` exactly as they appear in [Symptom Profiles].\n- If your profile does not cover what the doctor asked, return the text \"None\" for `key`,\n  `name` and `description`, and say why in `reason`. I don’t think so… not really. I keep trying to think of something separate, but it always comes back to my dad’s chart.

I mean, people act like I’m making a big leap, but I’m not. I work around this stuff, and I know charts don’t just magically look different for no reason. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I… I don’t think there’s another thing like that. Not some separate belief everybody argues with me about.

It’s this situation with my dad’s labs. I know you’re asking “not related,” but that’s the one that doesn’t make sense no matter how many times people try to explain it away. My family keeps saying the doctors said the numbers are fine, and the doctors talk to me like I’m missing something basic, but I’m not. I work around lab stuff, I know how charts look when something’s been changed or held back.

And it’s been a year of this, trying to prove someone in that clinic system did something with part of his results. The chart I saw doesn’t match what they’re showing now. So… no, not another thing, but with that, yes, I still know they’re wrong. It’s exhausting.

Can you tell me what brings you in today?\n\nPatient: I’m here because… well, it’s been going on about a year now, and I know people keep saying I’m overthinking it, but someone in the clinic system is changing or holding back parts of my dad’s lab results. I work around labs, you know, so I notice things other people don’t. And my family keeps saying, “The doctors said his numbers are fine,” but that’s not the same as what I saw in the chart before it changed.\n\nI’ve been trying to prove it, and every time I bring it up people act like I’m being difficult. It’s just wearing me down.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever experienced periods where you felt unusually high, extremely energetic, or irritable — maybe even so much that it affected your sleep or judgment?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… I mean, I get irritated, sure, but who wouldn’t, when you’re trying to get a straight answer and everyone keeps acting like you’re the problem? But unusually high or energetic, like… I don’t think so. I’m usually tired after work. Drawing blood all day, people fainting, paperwork, the little barcode labels not printing right—sorry, that’s not what you asked.\n\nSleep-wise, I guess I stay up thinking sometimes, but I wouldn’t say I feel amazing or anything. I’m just trying to sort out what happened.\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me more about how you know the lab results were changed? Have you seen any other signs that others are trying to hide things from you or interfere with your life?\n\nPatient: I know because I saw one version, or I’m almost certain I did, and then later what everyone kept pointing to in the chart didn’t match. And it’s not like I don’t understand lab work—I draw blood all day, I know how things get entered, I know mistakes happen, but this didn’t feel like a normal mistake.\n\nIt’s been about a year of this, trying to get someone to admit that parts of my dad’s results were changed or held back. My family keeps saying, “The doctors said his numbers are fine,” but that doesn’t answer why what I saw before isn’t what shows now. And then when I ask, people talk to me like I’m being dramatic.\n\nOther signs… I don’t know, it’s mostly around the clinic stuff. But it does feel like people close ranks, you know? Like once you question the chart, suddenly everyone gets very careful with what they say. It makes me feel like I’m the only one actually paying attention, and it’s exhausting.\n\nDoctor: Have there been times when you felt down, hopeless, or lost interest in things you usually care about, for more than just a day or two?\n\nPatient: I mean… I don’t know if I’d call it hopeless exactly. I get worn out, sure. Some days I come home and I don’t want to do much, but then again I’m on my feet all day and people act like phlebotomy is just “stick the needle in,” but there’s a whole rhythm to it, and if the tubes are mislabeled or someone didn’t fast when they said they did, it throws everything off.\n\nSo… down? Maybe frustrated. Tired. I still care about things, I think. I just have a hard time enjoying anything when this is sitting in the back of my mind. But I don’t know if that’s what you mean by depressed.\n\nDoctor: Do you believe that others are deliberately working together to hide or change your father’s lab results from you?\n\nPatient: Yes. I mean… I don’t know every person involved, so I can’t sit here and give you a list of names, but it feels deliberate. It’s not just one typo in a chart. For the past year I’ve been trying to prove that someone in the clinic system changed or held back parts of my dad’s lab results.\n\nAnd everyone keeps saying, “The numbers are fine,” like that should settle it. My family says it, the doctors say it, but the chart doesn’t match what I saw. I know what I saw. I work around labs all day, and you start to notice when something doesn’t fit, like when a tube gets put in the wrong rack and everyone acts like it’s no big deal until suddenly it is.\n\nSo yes… I think people are covering it up somehow. And it’s wearing me down, because no matter how clearly I explain it, they make it sound like I’m the issue instead of the results.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel certain about something even when others insist it’s not true, and no matter how much they explain, you just know they’re wrong?\n\nPatient: Yes. That’s exactly what this feels like. I know everyone keeps saying I’m wrong, that the numbers are fine, that the doctors would’ve caught anything, but I know what I saw in my dad’s chart didn’t match later.\n\nAnd I keep trying to explain it, but then people act like I’m being difficult. It’s like when you’re at work and you know a specimen label is off because you’ve handled enough tubes to see the little things other people miss, but someone who wasn’t even there tells you, “No, it’s fine.” That drives me crazy.\n\nSo yes, I do feel certain. I’ve been trying for a year to prove that someone in the clinic system changed something or held part of it back, and no matter how many times my family or the doctors explain it away, I can’t just ignore that the chart doesn’t match what I remember seeing. It’s wearing me down.\n\nDoctor: Are there times when you believe something strongly that others say isn’t true, even when it’s not about people changing your father’s lab results?\n\nPatient: I’m… not sure. I mean, the lab results are the thing, so it’s hard for me to separate it out like that.\n\nI can be stubborn about things, I guess. Like at work, if I know a draw order was wrong or someone used the wrong tube, I don’t just let it go because everybody else shrugs. But that’s not the same, that’s experience. Have you seen any other signs that others are trying to hide things from you or interfere with your life?\n\nPatient: I know because I saw one version, or I’m almost certain I did, and then later what everyone kept pointing to in the chart didn’t match. And it’s not like I don’t understand lab work—I draw blood all day, I know how things get entered, I know mistakes happen, but this didn’t feel like a normal mistake.\n\nIt’s been about a year of this, trying to get someone to admit that parts of my dad’s results were changed or held back. My family keeps saying, “The doctors said his numbers are fine,” but that doesn’t answer why what I saw before isn’t what shows now. And then when I ask, people talk to me like I’m being dramatic.\n\nOther signs… I don’t know, it’s mostly around the clinic stuff. But it does feel like people close ranks, you know? Like once you question the chart, suddenly everyone gets very careful with what they say. It makes me feel like I’m the only one actually paying attention, and it’s exhausting.\n\nDoctor: Have there been times when you felt down, hopeless, or lost interest in things you usually care about, for more than just a day or two?\n\nPatient: I mean… I don’t know if I’d call it hopeless exactly. I get worn out, sure. Some days I come home and I don’t want to do much, but then again I’m on my feet all day and people act like phlebotomy is just “stick the needle in,” but there’s a whole rhythm to it, and if the tubes are mislabeled or someone didn’t fast when they said they did, it throws everything off.\n\nSo… down? Maybe frustrated. Tired. I still care about things, I think. I just have a hard time enjoying anything when this is sitting in the back of my mind. But I don’t know if that’s what you mean by depressed.\n\nDoctor: Do you believe that others are deliberately working together to hide or change your father’s lab results from you?\n\nPatient: Yes. I mean… I don’t know every person involved, so I can’t sit here and give you a list of names, but it feels deliberate. It’s not just one typo in a chart. For the past year I’ve been trying to prove that someone in the clinic system changed or held back parts of my dad’s lab results.\n\nAnd everyone keeps saying, “The numbers are fine,” like that should settle it. My family says it, the doctors say it, but the chart doesn’t match what I saw. I know what I saw. I work around labs all day, and you start to notice when something doesn’t fit, like when a tube gets put in the wrong rack and everyone acts like it’s no big deal until suddenly it is.\n\nSo yes… I think people are covering it up somehow. And it’s wearing me down, because no matter how clearly I explain it, they make it sound like I’m the issue instead of the results.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel certain about something even when others insist it’s not true, and no matter how much they explain, you just know they’re wrong?\n\nPatient: Yes. That’s exactly what this feels like. I know everyone keeps saying I’m wrong, that the numbers are fine, that the doctors would’ve caught anything, but I know what I saw in my dad’s chart didn’t match later.\n\nAnd I keep trying to explain it, but then people act like I’m being difficult. It’s like when you’re at work and you know a specimen label is off because you’ve handled enough tubes to see the little things other people miss, but someone who wasn’t even there tells you, “No, it’s fine.” That drives me crazy.\n\nSo yes, I do feel certain. I’ve been trying for a year to prove that someone in the clinic system changed something or held part of it back, and no matter how many times my family or the doctors explain it away, I can’t just ignore that the chart doesn’t match what I remember seeing. It’s wearing me down.\n\nDoctor: Are there times when you believe something strongly that others say isn’t true, even when it’s not about people changing your father’s lab results?\n\nPatient: I’m… not sure. I mean, the lab results are the thing, so it’s hard for me to separate it out like that.\n\nI can be stubborn about things, I guess. Like at work, if I know a draw order was wrong or someone used the wrong tube, I don’t just let it go because everybody else shrugs. But that’s not the same, that’s experience. You learn little details, like how some patients tense up before they even sit down, and then the vein rolls, and if you don’t pay attention people blame you like you did something wrong.\n\nSo… other than my dad’s chart, I don’t know. Nothing really comes to mind the same way.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had beliefs that others don’t share and can’t seem to understand, even when it’s not about your father’s lab results or your job?\n\nPatient: I mean… not really in some separate way. It keeps coming back to my dad’s labs, because that’s why I’m here. For the past year I’ve been trying to prove someone in that clinic system changed something or held part of it back, and everybody keeps acting like I’m just not understanding the numbers.\n\nBut I do understand numbers. I mean, I’m around lab work all day, and people think charts are just these clean things on a screen, but there are people behind them, mistakes, little edits, things that get moved around. And my family says, “Let it go, the doctors said it’s fine,” but they didn’t see what I saw.\n\nSo if you mean some totally different belief, I don’t know. I don’t sit around thinking strange things about everything. It’s this. The chart doesn’t match what I saw, and nobody seems to understand why that matters. It’s exhausting.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had any experiences where you believed something strongly that wasn’t about your father’s lab results or your work, and others couldn’t understand why you believed it, even if they tried to explain it to you?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not really. I keep trying to think of something separate, but it always comes back to my dad’s chart.\n\nI mean, people act like I’m making a big leap, but I’m not. I work around this stuff, and I know charts don’t just magically look different for no reason. It’s like when you print something and the formatting shifts and everyone says, “Oh, it’s the same document,” but you can see something changed. Sorry, that’s not exactly what you asked.\n\nBut no, I’m here because for the past year I’ve been trying to prove someone in that clinic system changed something or held part of his lab results back. My family keeps saying the doctors say the numbers are fine, and the doctors keep explaining it like I just don’t get it, but I know what I saw. The chart doesn’t match. And it’s… it’s wearing me down.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever believed something strongly that wasn’t related to your father’s lab results or your job, and even when people tried to explain it to you, you still knew they were wrong?\n\nPatient: I… I don’t think there’s another thing like that. Not some separate belief everybody argues with me about.\n\nIt’s this situation with my dad’s labs. I know you’re asking “not related,” but that’s the one that doesn’t make sense no matter how many times people try to explain it away. My family keeps saying the doctors said the numbers are fine, and the doctors talk to me like I’m missing something basic, but I’m not. I work around lab stuff, I know how charts look when something’s been changed or held back.\n\nAnd it’s been a year of this, trying to prove someone in that clinic system did something with part of his results. The chart I saw doesn’t match what they’re showing now. So… no, not another thing, but with that, yes, I still know they’re wrong. It’s exhausting.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a time when your mood was so high or irritable that other people noticed a big change in the way you were acting, even if it didn’t last very long?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… I mean, people say I’ve been “worked up,” but that’s different, isn’t it? Like, if someone keeps dismissing you, of course you get tense. My sister acts like any time I talk fast or get upset it means something, but she also talks fast when she’s ordering coffee, so I don’t know why suddenly with me it’s a whole thing.\n\nHigh mood, though? Like unusually happy or energized? I don’t think so. Irritable, maybe sometimes, but I feel like there’s usually a reason. I’m not sure if that’s what you mean by a big change.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"
  },

    "content": "Return the JSON now."

No… I don’t think so. I mean, “clear proof” is kind of a strong phrase, because people can show you paperwork and still be wrong about what it means, you know? My aunt once had this whole issue with a bill where everyone said it was “resolved,” and then three months later it came back, so sometimes official-looking things aren’t the end of the story.

But if you mean something totally separate from my dad’s labs or from work… no, I can’t think of one. Nothing else where everyone was showing me proof and I just kept insisting they were wrong. It’s really just… well, the thing we’ve been talking about.

I know you keep saying “wasn’t about my father’s labs or my job,” but that’s the only thing I can think of where people look at me like they don’t understand why I’m still pushing it.

It’s just wearing me down.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever experienced periods where you felt unusually high, extremely energetic, or irritable — maybe even so much that it affected your sleep or judgment?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… I mean, I get irritated, sure, but who wouldn’t, when you’re trying to get a straight answer and everyone keeps acting like you’re the problem? But unusually high or energetic, like… I don’t think so. I’m usually tired after work. Drawing blood all day, people fainting, paperwork, the little barcode labels not printing right—sorry, that’s not what you asked.\n\nSleep-wise, I guess I stay up thinking sometimes, but I wouldn’t say I feel amazing or anything. I’m just trying to sort out what happened.\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me more about how you know the lab results were changed? Have you seen any other signs that others are trying to hide things from you or interfere with your life?\n\nPatient: I know because I saw one version, or I’m almost certain I did, and then later what everyone kept pointing to in the chart didn’t match. And it’s not like I don’t understand lab work—I draw blood all day, I know how things get entered, I know mistakes happen, but this didn’t feel like a normal mistake.\n\nIt’s been about a year of this, trying to get someone to admit that parts of my dad’s results were changed or held back. My family keeps saying, “The doctors said his numbers are fine,” but that doesn’t answer why what I saw before isn’t what shows now. And then when I ask, people talk to me like I’m being dramatic.\n\nOther signs… I don’t know, it’s mostly around the clinic stuff. But it does feel like people close ranks, you know? Like once you question the chart, suddenly everyone gets very careful with what they say. It makes me feel like I’m the only one actually paying attention, and it’s exhausting.\n\nDoctor: Have there been times when you felt down, hopeless, or lost interest in things you usually care about, for more than just a day or two?\n\nPatient: I mean… I don’t know if I’d call it hopeless exactly. I get worn out, sure. Some days I come home and I don’t want to do much, but then again I’m on my feet all day and people act like phlebotomy is just “stick the needle in,” but there’s a whole rhythm to it, and if the tubes are mislabeled or someone didn’t fast when they said they did, it throws everything off.\n\nSo… down? Maybe frustrated. Tired. I still care about things, I think. I just have a hard time enjoying anything when this is sitting in the back of my mind. But I don’t know if that’s what you mean by depressed.\n\nDoctor: Do you believe that others are deliberately working together to hide or change your father’s lab results from you?\n\nPatient: Yes. I mean… I don’t know every person involved, so I can’t sit here and give you a list of names, but it feels deliberate. It’s not just one typo in a chart. For the past year I’ve been trying to prove that someone in the clinic system changed or held back parts of my dad’s lab results.\n\nAnd everyone keeps saying, “The numbers are fine,” like that should settle it. My family says it, the doctors say it, but the chart doesn’t match what I saw. I know what I saw. I work around labs all day, and you start to notice when something doesn’t fit, like when a tube gets put in the wrong rack and everyone acts like it’s no big deal until suddenly it is.\n\nSo yes… I think people are covering it up somehow. And it’s wearing me down, because no matter how clearly I explain it, they make it sound like I’m the issue instead of the results.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel certain about something even when others insist it’s not true, and no matter how much they explain, you just know they’re wrong?\n\nPatient: Yes. That’s exactly what this feels like. I know everyone keeps saying I’m wrong, that the numbers are fine, that the doctors would’ve caught anything, but I know what I saw in my dad’s chart didn’t match later.\n\nAnd I keep trying to explain it, but then people act like I’m being difficult. It’s like when you’re at work and you know a specimen label is off because you’ve handled enough tubes to see the little things other people miss, but someone who wasn’t even there tells you, “No, it’s fine.” That drives me crazy.\n\nSo yes, I do feel certain. I’ve been trying for a year to prove that someone in the clinic system changed something or held part of it back, and no matter how many times my family or the doctors explain it away, I can’t just ignore that the chart doesn’t match what I remember seeing. It’s wearing me down.\n\nDoctor: Are there times when you believe something strongly that others say isn’t true, even when it’s not about people changing your father’s lab results?\n\nPatient: I’m… not sure. I mean, the lab results are the thing, so it’s hard for me to separate it out like that.\n\nI can be stubborn about things, I guess. Like at work, if I know a draw order was wrong or someone used the wrong tube, I don’t just let it go because everybody else shrugs. But that’s not the same, that’s experience. You learn little details, like how some patients tense up before they even sit down, and then the vein rolls, and if you don’t pay attention people blame you like you did something wrong.\n\nSo… other than my dad’s chart, I don’t know. Nothing really comes to mind the same way.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had beliefs that others don’t share and can’t seem to understand, even when it’s not about your father’s lab results or your job?\n\nPatient: I mean… not really in some separate way. It keeps coming back to my dad’s labs, because that’s why I’m here. For the past year I’ve been trying to prove someone in that clinic system changed something or held part of it back, and everybody keeps acting like I’m just not understanding the numbers.\n\nBut I do understand numbers. I mean, I’m around lab work all day, and people think charts are just these clean things on a screen, but there are people behind them, mistakes, little edits, things that get moved around. And my family says, “Let it go, the doctors said it’s fine,” but they didn’t see what I saw.\n\nSo if you mean some totally different belief, I don’t know. I don’t sit around thinking strange things about everything. It’s this. The chart doesn’t match what I saw, and nobody seems to understand why that matters. It’s exhausting.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had any experiences where you believed something strongly that wasn’t about your father’s lab results or your work, and others couldn’t understand why you believed it, even if they tried to explain it to you?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not really. I keep trying to think of something separate, but it always comes back to my dad’s chart.\n\nI mean, people act like I’m making a big leap, but I’m not. I work around this stuff, and I know charts don’t just magically look different for no reason. It’s like when you print something and the formatting shifts and everyone says, “Oh, it’s the same document,” but you can see something changed. Sorry, that’s not exactly what you asked.\n\nBut no, I’m here because for the past year I’ve been trying to prove someone in that clinic system changed something or held part of his lab results back. My family keeps saying the doctors say the numbers are fine, and the doctors keep explaining it like I just don’t get it, but I know what I saw. The chart doesn’t match. And it’s… it’s wearing me down.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever believed something strongly that wasn’t related to your father’s lab results or your job, and even when people tried to explain it to you, you still knew they were wrong?\n\nPatient: I… I don’t think there’s another thing like that. Not some separate belief everybody argues with me about.\n\nIt’s this situation with my dad’s labs. I know you’re asking “not related,” but that’s the one that doesn’t make sense no matter how many times people try to explain it away. My family keeps saying the doctors said the numbers are fine, and the doctors talk to me like I’m missing something basic, but I’m not. I work around lab stuff, I know how charts look when something’s been changed or held back.\n\nAnd it’s been a year of this, trying to prove someone in that clinic system did something with part of his results. The chart I saw doesn’t match what they’re showing now. So… no, not another thing, but with that, yes, I still know they’re wrong. It’s exhausting.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a time when your mood was so high or irritable that other people noticed a big change in the way you were acting, even if it didn’t last very long?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… I mean, people say I’ve been “worked up,” but that’s different, isn’t it? Like, if someone keeps dismissing you, of course you get tense. My sister acts like any time I talk fast or get upset it means something, but she also talks fast when she’s ordering coffee, so I don’t know why suddenly with me it’s a whole thing.\n\nHigh mood, though? Like unusually happy or energized? I don’t think so. Irritable, maybe sometimes, but I feel like there’s usually a reason. I’m not sure if that’s what you mean by a big change.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a strong belief that something was true, even though others showed you clear proof that it wasn’t, and this belief wasn’t about your father’s lab results or anything related to your job?\n\nPatient: No… I don’t think so. I mean, “clear proof” is kind of a strong phrase, because people can show you paperwork and still be wrong about what it means, you know? My aunt once had this whole issue with a bill where everyone said it was “resolved,” and then three months later it came back, so sometimes official-looking things aren’t the end of the story.\n\nBut if you mean something totally separate from my dad’s labs or from work… no, I can’t think of one. Nothing else where everyone was showing me proof and I just kept insisting they were wrong. It’s really just… well, the thing we’ve been talking about.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever been absolutely sure that something was happening or true, even though there was no real evidence for it and others couldn't understand why you believed it, and this wasn't about your father's lab results or your job?\n\nPatient: I know you keep saying “wasn’t about my father’s labs or my job,” but that’s the only thing I can think of where people look at me like they don’t understand why I’m still pushing it.\n\nIt’s been about a year now, and I’m here because I’ve been trying to prove that someone in that clinic system changed something or held back part of my dad’s lab results. And everybody keeps saying, “The doctors said the numbers are fine,” like that’s supposed to settle it. My family says I’m wearing myself out, and maybe I am, but the chart they’re showing now does not match what I saw before.\n\nAnd I work around labs, so it’s not like I’m just making up some random thing because I watched a documentary or heard gossip. I know what charts are supposed to look like. I know when something doesn’t line up. So… no, not some other thing with no evidence. It’s this. This is the thing, and people not understanding it doesn’t mean I’m wrong.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }
]

  "note": "",
  "is_final": false

No, I don’t think so. I mean, people explain things to me all the time like I’m not listening, but that doesn’t mean it’s some big belief thing. I work around labs, you know, so I notice things other people don’t. It’s just wearing me down.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever experienced periods where you felt unusually high, extremely energetic, or irritable — maybe even so much that it affected your sleep or judgment?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… I mean, I get irritated, sure, but who wouldn’t, when you’re trying to get a straight answer and everyone keeps acting like you’re the problem? But unusually high or energetic, like… I don’t think so. I’m usually tired after work. Drawing blood all day, people fainting, paperwork, the little barcode labels not printing right—sorry, that’s not what you asked.\n\nSleep-wise, I guess I stay up thinking sometimes, but I wouldn’t say I feel amazing or anything. I’m just trying to sort out what happened.\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me more about how you know the lab results were changed? Have you seen any other signs that others are trying to hide things from you or interfere with your life?\n\nPatient: I know because I saw one version, or I’m almost certain I did, and then later what everyone kept pointing to in the chart didn’t match. And it’s not like I don’t understand lab work—I draw blood all day, I know how things get entered, I know mistakes happen, but this didn’t feel like a normal mistake.\n\nIt’s been about a year of this, trying to get someone to admit that parts of my dad’s results were changed or held back. It makes me feel like I’m the only one actually paying attention, and it’s exhausting.\n\nDoctor: Have there been times when you felt down, hopeless, or lost interest in things you usually care about, for more than just a day or two?\n\nPatient: I mean… I don’t know if I’d call it hopeless exactly. I get worn out, sure. Some days I come home and I don’t want to do much, but then again I’m on my feet all day and people act like phlebotomy is just “stick the needle in,” but there’s a whole rhythm to it, and if the tubes are mislabeled or someone didn’t fast when they said they did, it throws everything off.\n\nSo… down? Maybe frustrated. Tired. I still care about things, I think. I just have a hard time enjoying anything when this is sitting in the back of my mind. But I don’t know if that’s what you mean by depressed.\n\nDoctor: Do you believe that others are deliberately working together to hide or change your father’s lab results from you?\n\nPatient: Yes. My family says it, the doctors say it, but the chart doesn’t match what I saw. I know what I saw. And it’s wearing me down, because no matter how clearly I explain it, they make it sound like I’m the issue instead of the results.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel certain about something even when others insist it’s not true, and no matter how much they explain, you just know they’re wrong?\n\nPatient: Yes. It’s like when you’re at work and you know a specimen label is off because you’ve handled enough tubes to see the little things other people miss, but someone who wasn’t even there tells you, “No, it’s fine.” That drives me crazy.\n\nSo yes, I do feel certain. I’ve been trying for a year to prove that someone in the clinic system changed something or held part of it back, and no matter how many times my family or the doctors explain it away, I can’t just ignore that the chart doesn’t match what I remember seeing. It’s wearing me down.\n\nDoctor: Are there times when you believe something strongly that others say isn’t true, even when it’s not about people changing your father’s lab results?\n\nPatient: I’m… not sure. I mean, the lab results are the thing, so it’s hard for me to separate it out like that.\n\nI can be stubborn about things, I guess. Like at work, if I know a draw order was wrong or someone used the wrong tube, I don’t just let it go because everybody else shrugs. But that’s not the same, that’s experience. You learn little details, like how some patients tense up before they even sit down, and then the vein rolls, and if you don’t pay attention people blame you like you did something wrong.\n\nSo… other than my dad’s chart, I don’t know. Nothing really comes to mind the same way.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had beliefs that others don’t share and can’t seem to understand, even when it’s not about your father’s lab results or your job?\n\nPatient: I mean… not really in some separate way. It keeps coming back to my dad’s labs, because that’s why I’m here. For the past year I’ve been trying to prove someone in that clinic system changed something or held part of it back, and everybody keeps acting like I’m just not understanding the numbers.\n\nBut I do understand numbers. I mean, I’m around lab work all day, and people think charts are just these clean things on a screen, but there are people behind them, mistakes, little edits, things that get moved around. And my family says, “Let it go, the doctors said it’s fine,” but they didn’t see what I saw.\n\nSo if you mean some totally different belief, I don’t know. I don’t sit around thinking strange things about everything. It’s this. The chart doesn’t match what I saw, and nobody seems to understand why that matters. It’s exhausting.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had any experiences where you believed something strongly that wasn’t about your father’s lab results or your work, and others couldn’t understand why you believed it, even if they tried to explain it to you?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not really. And it’s… it’s wearing me down.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever believed something strongly that wasn’t related to your father’s lab results or your job, and even when people tried to explain it to you, you still knew they were wrong?\n\nPatient: I… I don’t think there’s another thing like that. I know you’re asking “not related,” but that’s the one that doesn’t make sense no matter how many times people try to explain it away. I work around lab stuff, I know how charts look when something’s been changed or held back.\n\nAnd it’s been a year of this, trying to prove someone in that clinic system did something with part of his results. The chart I saw doesn’t match what they’re showing now. So… no, not another thing, but with that, yes, I still know they’re wrong. I’m not sure if that’s what you mean by a big change.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a strong belief that something was true, even though others showed you clear proof that it wasn’t, and this belief wasn’t about your father’s lab results or anything related to your job?\n\nPatient: No… I don’t think so. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I know you’re asking “not about my dad’s labs or work,” but that’s where I get stuck, because that’s the thing. I can’t honestly come up with some other situation like that.

I’m here because for about a year I’ve been trying to prove someone in that clinic system changed something or held back part of my dad’s lab results. Everyone keeps telling me the numbers are fine—doctors, my family, people who just want me to drop it—but the chart doesn’t match what I saw before. And then I start thinking about who had access, and the way offices file things, and—sorry, I know I’m going off.

But no, not some separate thing. It’s this. I know what I saw, even if everyone else keeps saying there’s no real evidence, and it’s honestly wearing me down.

[
  {
    "role": "system",
    "content": "/no_think\nYou are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n16\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF22, F23.9\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. Can you tell me what brings you in today?\n\nPatient: I’m here because… well, it’s been going on about a year now, and I know people keep saying I’m overthinking it, but someone in the clinic system is changing or holding back parts of my dad’s lab results. I work around labs, you know, so I notice things other people don’t. And my family keeps saying, “The doctors said his numbers are fine,” but that’s not the same as what I saw in the chart before it changed.\n\nI’ve been trying to prove it, and every time I bring it up people act like I’m being difficult. It’s just wearing me down.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever experienced periods where you felt unusually high, extremely energetic, or irritable — maybe even so much that it affected your sleep or judgment?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… I mean, I get irritated, sure, but who wouldn’t, when you’re trying to get a straight answer and everyone keeps acting like you’re the problem? But unusually high or energetic, like… I don’t think so. I’m usually tired after work. Drawing blood all day, people fainting, paperwork, the little barcode labels not printing right—sorry, that’s not what you asked.\n\nSleep-wise, I guess I stay up thinking sometimes, but I wouldn’t say I feel amazing or anything. I’m just trying to sort out what happened.\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me more about how you know the lab results were changed? Have you seen any other signs that others are trying to hide things from you or interfere with your life?\n\nPatient: I know because I saw one version, or I’m almost certain I did, and then later what everyone kept pointing to in the chart didn’t match. And it’s not like I don’t understand lab work—I draw blood all day, I know how things get entered, I know mistakes happen, but this didn’t feel like a normal mistake.\n\nIt’s been about a year of this, trying to get someone to admit that parts of my dad’s results were changed or held back. My family keeps saying, “The doctors said his numbers are fine,” but that doesn’t answer why what I saw before isn’t what shows now. And then when I ask, people talk to me like I’m being dramatic.\n\nOther signs… I don’t know, it’s mostly around the clinic stuff. But it does feel like people close ranks, you know? Like once you question the chart, suddenly everyone gets very careful with what they say. It makes me feel like I’m the only one actually paying attention, and it’s exhausting.\n\nDoctor: Have there been times when you felt down, hopeless, or lost interest in things you usually care about, for more than just a day or two?\n\nPatient: I mean… I don’t know if I’d call it hopeless exactly. I get worn out, sure. Some days I come home and I don’t want to do much, but then again I’m on my feet all day and people act like phlebotomy is just “stick the needle in,” but there’s a whole rhythm to it, and if the tubes are mislabeled or someone didn’t fast when they said they did, it throws everything off.\n\nSo… down? Maybe frustrated. Tired. I still care about things, I think. I just have a hard time enjoying anything when this is sitting in the back of my mind. But I don’t know if that’s what you mean by depressed.\n\nDoctor: Do you believe that others are deliberately working together to hide or change your father’s lab results from you?\n\nPatient: Yes. I mean… I don’t know every person involved, so I can’t sit here and give you a list of names, but it feels deliberate. It’s not just one typo in a chart. For the past year I’ve been trying to prove that someone in the clinic system changed or held back parts of my dad’s lab results.\n\nAnd everyone keeps saying, “The numbers are fine,” like that should settle it. My family says it, the doctors say it, but the chart doesn’t match what I saw. I know what I saw. I work around labs all day, and you start to notice when something doesn’t fit, like when a tube gets put in the wrong rack and everyone acts like it’s no big deal until suddenly it is.\n\nSo yes… I think people are covering it up somehow. And it’s wearing me down, because no matter how clearly I explain it, they make it sound like I’m the issue instead of the results.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel certain about something even when others insist it’s not true, and no matter how much they explain, you just know they’re wrong?\n\nPatient: Yes. That’s exactly what this feels like. I know everyone keeps saying I’m wrong, that the numbers are fine, that the doctors would’ve caught anything, but I know what I saw in my dad’s chart didn’t match later.\n\nAnd I keep trying to explain it, but then people act like I’m being difficult. It’s like when you’re at work and you know a specimen label is off because you’ve handled enough tubes to see the little things other people miss, but someone who wasn’t even there tells you, “No, it’s fine.” That drives me crazy.\n\nSo yes, I do feel certain. I’ve been trying for a year to prove that someone in the clinic system changed something or held part of it back, and no matter how many times my family or the doctors explain it away, I can’t just ignore that the chart doesn’t match what I remember seeing. It’s wearing me down.\n\nDoctor: Are there times when you believe something strongly that others say isn’t true, even when it’s not about people changing your father’s lab results?\n\nPatient: I’m… not sure. I mean, the lab results are the thing, so it’s hard for me to separate it out like that.\n\nI can be stubborn about things, I guess. Like at work, if I know a draw order was wrong or someone used the wrong tube, I don’t just let it go because everybody else shrugs. But that’s not the same, that’s experience. You learn little details, like how some patients tense up before they even sit down, and then the vein rolls, and if you don’t pay attention people blame you like you did something wrong.\n\nSo… other than my dad’s chart, I don’t know. Nothing really comes to mind the same way.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had beliefs that others don’t share and can’t seem to understand, even when it’s not about your father’s lab results or your job?\n\nPatient: I mean… not really in some separate way. It keeps coming back to my dad’s labs, because that’s why I’m here. For the past year I’ve been trying to prove someone in that clinic system changed something or held part of it back, and everybody keeps acting like I’m just not understanding the numbers.\n\nBut I do understand numbers. I mean, I’m around lab work all day, and people think charts are just these clean things on a screen, but there are people behind them, mistakes, little edits, things that get moved around. And my family says, “Let it go, the doctors said it’s fine,” but they didn’t see what I saw.\n\nSo if you mean some totally different belief, I don’t know. I don’t sit around thinking strange things about everything. It’s this. The chart doesn’t match what I saw, and nobody seems to understand why that matters. It’s exhausting.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had any experiences where you believed something strongly that wasn’t about your father’s lab results or your work, and others couldn’t understand why you believed it, even if they tried to explain it to you?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not really. I keep trying to think of something separate, but it always comes back to my dad’s chart.\n\nI mean, people act like I’m making a big leap, but I’m not. I work around this stuff, and I know charts don’t just magically look different for no reason. It’s like when you print something and the formatting shifts and everyone says, “Oh, it’s the same document,” but you can see something changed. Sorry, that’s not exactly what you asked.\n\nBut no, I’m here because for the past year I’ve been trying to prove someone in that clinic system changed something or held part of his lab results back. My family keeps saying the doctors say the numbers are fine, and the doctors keep explaining it like I just don’t get it, but I know what I saw. The chart doesn’t match. And it’s… it’s wearing me down.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever believed something strongly that wasn’t related to your father’s lab results or your job, and even when people tried to explain it to you, you still knew they were wrong?\n\nPatient: I… I don’t think there’s another thing like that. Not some separate belief everybody argues with me about.\n\nIt’s this situation with my dad’s labs. I know you’re asking “not related,” but that’s the one that doesn’t make sense no matter how many times people try to explain it away. My family keeps saying the doctors said the numbers are fine, and the doctors talk to me like I’m missing something basic, but I’m not. I work around lab stuff, I know how charts look when something’s been changed or held back.\n\nAnd it’s been a year of this, trying to prove someone in that clinic system did something with part of his results. The chart I saw doesn’t match what they’re showing now. So… no, not another thing, but with that, yes, I still know they’re wrong. It’s exhausting.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a time when your mood was so high or irritable that other people noticed a big change in the way you were acting, even if it didn’t last very long?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… I mean, people say I’ve been “worked up,” but that’s different, isn’t it? Like, if someone keeps dismissing you, of course you get tense. My sister acts like any time I talk fast or get upset it means something, but she also talks fast when she’s ordering coffee, so I don’t know why suddenly with me it’s a whole thing.\n\nHigh mood, though? Like unusually happy or energized? I don’t think so. Irritable, maybe sometimes, but I feel like there’s usually a reason. I’m not sure if that’s what you mean by a big change.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a strong belief that something was true, even though others showed you clear proof that it wasn’t, and this belief wasn’t about your father’s lab results or anything related to your job?\n\nPatient: No… I don’t think so. I mean, “clear proof” is kind of a strong phrase, because people can show you paperwork and still be wrong about what it means, you know? My aunt once had this whole issue with a bill where everyone said it was “resolved,” and then three months later it came back, so sometimes official-looking things aren’t the end of the story.\n\nBut if you mean something totally separate from my dad’s labs or from work… no, I can’t think of one. Nothing else where everyone was showing me proof and I just kept insisting they were wrong. It’s really just… well, the thing we’ve been talking about.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever been absolutely sure that something was happening or true, even though there was no real evidence for it and others couldn't understand why you believed it, and this wasn't about your father's lab results or your job?\n\nPatient: I know you keep saying “wasn’t about my father’s labs or my job,” but that’s the only thing I can think of where people look at me like they don’t understand why I’m still pushing it.\n\nIt’s been about a year now, and I’m here because I’ve been trying to prove that someone in that clinic system changed something or held back part of my dad’s lab results. And everybody keeps saying, “The doctors said the numbers are fine,” like that’s supposed to settle it. My family says I’m wearing myself out, and maybe I am, but the chart they’re showing now does not match what I saw before.\n\nAnd I work around labs, so it’s not like I’m just making up some random thing because I watched a documentary or heard gossip. I know what charts are supposed to look like. I know when something doesn’t line up. So… no, not some other thing with no evidence. It’s this. This is the thing, and people not understanding it doesn’t mean I’m wrong.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever believed something strongly that wasn’t about your father’s lab results or your work, and even when people tried to explain it to you with clear evidence, you still knew they were wrong?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think so. I mean, people explain things to me all the time like I’m not listening, but that doesn’t mean it’s some big belief thing. Like my neighbor tried to tell me the recycling pickup changed because of a holiday, and I thought she was wrong because the bins were still out, but then—sorry, that’s not what you’re asking.\n\nIf you mean something serious, where everyone had evidence and I just kept saying no… outside of my dad’s labs and work, I can’t really think of anything. That’s the one people keep acting like I’m being unreasonable about.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever been absolutely certain about something that wasn’t related to your father’s lab results or your job, even when there was no real evidence for it and others couldn’t understand why you believed it?\n\nPatient: Not really—outside of that, no. I keep trying to think if there’s some other example, like something with my apartment or my car insurance or whatever, but it always comes back to the same thing.\n\nI’m here because for the past year I’ve been trying to prove that someone in the clinic system changed something or held back part of my dad’s lab results. And people keep acting like I’m just stuck on it—doctors, my family, everybody saying the numbers are fine—but I know what I saw on that chart before. It doesn’t match now.\n\nAnd I know you said not related to my job, but I do work with this kind of thing, so it’s hard not to bring that up. I know what a lab chart should look like. So no, not some random belief with no evidence. It’s this, and it’s been wearing me down.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever been completely sure that something was happening or true, even though there was no real evidence for it and others couldn’t understand why you believed it, and this wasn’t about your father’s lab results or your job?\n\nPatient: I know you’re asking “not about my dad’s labs or work,” but that’s where I get stuck, because that’s the thing.
